11 unresolved mysteries FROM must answer in 5th and final season

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

From the town's origins to red lightning, 11 open FROM mysteries the final season must settle, and which ones the ending may not need.

Viewers of "From" have spent four seasons watching people trapped in a town nobody can leave, under rules nobody can explain, and each season has raised new questions faster than it has answered the old ones. Those questions now have a deadline. The series has been renewed for a fifth season that will be its last, ScreenRant reports, and that season is expected in 2027, according to Entertainment Weekly. After a long wait, the writers will have a single season to explain every unexplained rule, creature and power, or to decide to leave it alone. The open questions reach well beyond how the town began and into how it works from one night to the next. The number of them, more than any single riddle, is what keeps the ending uncertain.

The show will end at the length its makers first proposed, though that outcome was not settled along the way. ScreenRant reports that the series was pitched to MGM+ as five seasons and 50 episodes. In 2023, during the show's second season, executive producer Jeff Pinkner was weighing four, five or six seasons, according to Arizona Daily Star. The fifth and final season brings the series back to its original plan after a period when that plan was one option among several. Pinkner's view at the time was that when a series is built around its characters, the people making it can sense when it should stop. The makers will measure the ending by where the characters arrive, not by how many puzzles get closed. A fan's checklist and the finale may not match, and some threads may be answered only as far as the characters need them to be. That gap sets the fairest test for any open question: whether the ending depends on it, not whether it is intriguing.

FandomWire set out to find which open puzzles the show must solve to conclude its final season, from the red lightning to Julie's storywalking, the Boy in White and the Lake of Tears. Those puzzles are less separate than they look. In the fourth season's finale, the writers brought many of them into a single night and tied them to the same antagonist, so answering one may answer several. Some have already been partly settled on screen, while others grew more urgent after the town lost its protections, and "unresolved" means something different from one question to the next. Each slide that follows weighs what a question still hides and why the ending needs it answered.

Most of the show's riddles concern events inside Fromville, but this one concerns the town itself, and every other answer depends on it. Before the series ends, it has to say who made the town, what ties it to the world beyond and what becomes of the place and everything in it if the cycle ends, according to FandomWire. Those are the most basic facts about any place, and the show has yet to supply any of them. A finale can leave a side story unresolved, but a finale that never says what the town is would leave every other explanation without a basis. This is also the answer most likely to settle several questions at once, because the identity of the town's maker and the nature of its tie to the outside world would bear on nearly every mystery that grew out of them.

The confusion the residents feel is what drives the drama, and the people making the show treat that confusion as something to protect. Harold Perrineau, the actor who plays one of the town's leaders, has chosen not to find out how the series ends. "The torture (my character) is feeling is easier for me to embody if I really don’t know," Perrineau told the Arizona Daily Star. That gives the final season a harder job than delivering an explanation, since revealing what the town is will also end the uncertainty behind much of the characters' suffering.

The thing to watch, then, is not only who built Fromville but whether the show's makers reveal it in a way that gives that suffering a purpose rather than simply ending it.

The bargain behind Fromville is still one of the show's most basic open questions, and the final season will have to say who made it and how it works. FandomWire counts the sacrifice among the unanswered questions about the town's origins that the final season has to take up. Part of the trade is known: The original townspeople gave up seven children and received immortality in return, according to ComicBook.com. The town's founding was therefore a deliberate atrocity by a whole community, and any moral reckoning in the last season has to answer for it. Knowing the price is not the same as understanding the deal, though, and the show has yet to explain how the bargain was struck or what keeps it in force.

The fourth season's finale showed how little the residents understand about what the founders set in motion. Boyd and other townspeople uprooted the Bottle Tree, ComicBook.com reports, and a crimson lightning storm appeared over the town after the retrieval went wrong. The residents acted without knowing the rules behind the place, and they now face a storm none of them can explain. A question about the town's origins has become a pressing problem for the people living there now.

The residents still can't explain the bargain, so the way out may not lie in the children's story alone. The sacrifice now belongs to the larger struggle between the Man in Yellow and the Boy in White, and the final season may resolve it through that contest rather than as a separate puzzle.

For the ending, the real test is whether anyone breaks the bargain's hold on the town. A full account of how the founders struck it may matter less, and an ending built around the characters can deliver that kind of answer.

The show has already confirmed that people in Fromville come back, so the open question about reincarnation is no longer whether it happens but what it is for. FandomWire groups the question surrounding Tabitha and Jade with the town's origins and sacrifices. That grouping fits Tabitha, whom the previous season confirmed as a reincarnation of Miranda, according to Primetimer. Explaining the returns may also mean explaining the deaths that come with them, and whether the killings in the town follow a schedule.

The same family arrives in each cycle. Miranda came to town in the 1970s with her son, Victor, and her daughter, Eloise, Primetimer reports, and Tabitha arrived with one son and one daughter. Victor alone came through Miranda's cycle alive, and in an earlier cycle, the original Tabitha's daughter was sacrificed and became an Anghkooey child. In both cycles viewers know, a daughter was lost. If that pattern holds, Tabitha's daughter came into town already in danger, and the ending can't leave that unanswered.

FROM requires a person to die before being reborn, Primetimer writer Kinette Sumadia writes, and she uses that rule to argue that neither Victor nor the Boy in White is a reincarnation. By that reading, Victor never returned at all. He stayed. He also wants Ethan prepared to survive alone, which suggests he expects Miranda's outcome to repeat with Tabitha's family. Sumadia also connects Ethan to the Boy in White, a link the show hasn't confirmed.

The final season has to decide whether anyone can stop the cycle, because that determines whether Tabitha's children face the same split Miranda's did.

Fromville has a history of mass killing that nobody has accounted for, and that history may not be over. FandomWire lists the 1978 massacre as unexplained and says residents may face new purges each time they discover too much. That sets the logic of the show against its own characters. They stay alive by chasing answers, yet the more they uncover, the closer they may come to the next killing. The final season can't file the massacre away as backstory, because the residents face more danger with every mystery they solve.

The fourth season's finale has already shown what removing one resident looks like. Reel 360 reports that Sophia killed Elgin after he prayed, and his death reads as punishment for defying her rather than as bad luck. Sophia is the vessel for the Man in Yellow, so this killing leads straight to the show's central antagonist and not to a stray monster. She may also have been in town long before this season. The old photo Elgin found shows Sophia, which makes her a possible link between the deaths of the past and those of the present, even though nothing yet places her at the 1978 massacre.

The final season may never give the massacre a tidy explanation drawn from the past, and the writers could answer it with a fresh killing instead. Another round of deaths seems as likely a way to resolve the mystery as a scene set in 1978, though the show has committed to neither. The ending depends on this question because it decides whether the danger in Fromville comes from rules the characters can learn, or from one antagonist who punishes anyone who gets too close.

The bloodiest secret in Fromville may be settled not by finding out what happened in 1978, but by whether it happens again.

Two figures appear to stand outside the rules that trap everyone else in Fromville, and the show has explained neither of them. Nobody yet knows who the Man in Yellow and the Boy in White are or what either one wants, and the two differ sharply in how freely they act and how they help the residents, according to FandomWire. The Man in Yellow spent the fourth season disguised as Sophia, living among the residents without their knowing who he was, according to ScreenRant. The Boy in White, by contrast, offers only cryptic guidance, so the residents receive help they can't fully interpret. One of the two moves among the townspeople under a stolen identity, while the other stays at the edges of town.

The fourth season's finale turned these two separate puzzles into a single rivalry. Its final scene placed the Boy in White with Sophia just as Sophia was revealed as the Man in Yellow in disguise, ScreenRant reports, and the meeting was deliberate. Series creator and executive producer John Griffin told ScreenRant the scene was "confirmation that these two figures, who have operated in the background and whom we've only seen on the periphery, are absolutely aware of each other." From here on, any answer about what one of them is has to account for the other.

The difference in their freedom could reflect a choice or a limit, and the show hasn't said which. The Boy in White may keep his distance on purpose, or he may be unable to do more. ScreenRant reports that showrunner and executive producer Jeff Pinkner sees the story as taking shape as it goes rather than following a fixed course. The contest between the two figures is left for the final season to settle. The open question is also a practical one for the characters. How much help the residents can count on before the series ends depends on whether the Boy in White won't act or can't act.

Julie has walked away from the one power in town that lets a person move through the past, and nobody has ever explained how that power works. FandomWire lists her storywalking as unexplained, vanished or changed. ScreenRant reports that Julie gave up on storywalking in the fourth season's ninth episode. The show hasn't said whether the ability faded or changed or whether she simply stopped using it, so the open question now covers Julie as well as the power. With a single season left, the show has to bring the power back on purpose or knowingly let it go.

Whatever storywalking can do, it can't undo every loss in town. Julie can't reverse Jim's death, according to ScreenRant. That limit changes what is at stake each time she goes back. The power may matter more for what Julie could learn than for anything she could change. Casey Duby, a writer and critic at ScreenRant, suggests Julie could look into earlier cycles to expose the Man in Yellow's secrets. In a final season, knowing what happened before may be worth more to the survivors than any chance to erase it.

Julie's doubt now reaches beyond her own ability to the survivors' other plans for fighting back. She argued that Tabitha's plan was a trap, ScreenRant reports, and that suspicion now shapes whom the group is willing to believe. Her doubt places storywalking inside the struggle with the Man in Yellow. Julie has set aside a power that might show his past while the survivors argue over which guidance to follow.

If the ending explains what storywalking is and why Julie gave it up, it could explain a good deal about the Man in Yellow along the way.

In Fromville, characters who die do not necessarily leave the story. Residents the town has lost keep appearing again, from Father Khatri to Jim, and FandomWire counts these returns among the town's entities and supernatural powers, with no explanation for them confirmed. That places the visits closer to a force at work in the town than to grief or memory. Who sends the dead back is as open a question as who they are, and the answer matters more than any list of the names that reappear.

The fourth season's finale suggests the apparitions may belong to a larger mystery. Elgin found an old photo of a young woman who looked just like Sophia, and the finale then revealed that the Man in Yellow had been taking her form, according to Entertainment Weekly. At least one familiar face in town may therefore be borrowed from its past, and recognizing a face no longer proves who is behind it. If the Man in Yellow can take on the appearance of people from the town's history, the returning dead may be his instruments rather than ghosts acting on their own. In that case, the final season could settle the apparitions without addressing them directly, simply by explaining what the Man in Yellow is.

The ending still needs an answer, because the characters can no longer trust what they see. The figure behind Sophia's face can also cloud the minds of the living, and in the finale Sophia drugged Henry and left him hallucinating, according to Entertainment Weekly. Elgin, the resident who came closest to the truth about Sophia's face, died in the same finale, and he now stands among the dead whose possible return is an open question. Any sighting of him in the final season would raise the same doubt he was chasing. A vision in town can be false in two ways, with a dead face being worn or a living mind being clouded.

The finale does not need a catalog of every ghost. It does need a rule for when the dead are really themselves, because the characters cannot make their last choices without knowing whom to trust.

The residents' only proven weapon against a Creature works by rules the show has never explained. Boyd used blood worms to bring down the Smiley Creature, Primetimer reports, but what the worms and the blood did to Boyd and to Henry is still an open question, according to FandomWire. That makes the question a matter of survival, because the residents can't plan a final fight around a weapon whose rules none of them understand. Boyd can't simply strike again, either, because the worms were expelled from his body once he passed them on. Martin carried the worms before Boyd and died while transferring them, so the only man who might have said where they came from is gone. If the town is going to use the worms again, the last season may have to return to the question of where more of them could come from.

The steps of the transfer are known even though its workings are not. "My blood is your blood," Martin said as he handed the worms on, as reported by Primetimer, and Boyd repeated the vow almost word for word when he passed them to Smiley. Boyd copied what he had seen Martin do, but repeating the words is not the same as knowing why a Creature dies when the worms reach it. Until the show reveals those rules, any victory over the Creatures would look like luck rather than a payoff.

Fatima faces the same unknown from the other side, because nobody yet knows whether her change into something monstrous is permanent. The town's rules can destroy a Creature and can also remake a person, and the show has explained neither process.

The worms are a plot device, but Fatima is a character, and whether she can come back is the part of this mystery the final season can least afford to leave open.

Most of the town's open questions ask who or what is out there, but this one asks where. Nobody knows what part the lighthouse or the Lake of Tears plays in breaking the curse, or what would happen if someone brought bones to the lighthouse or the tower, according to FandomWire. That puts escape itself at stake. If the two places do figure in breaking the curse, the residents may be missing a step in the way out of town without knowing what that step does.

The lake entered the story in Ethan's dream in the first season's second episode, Primetimer reports, and for a long time afterward it remained only a dream image. The show never said what it was for, even as it added other mysteries around it. That changed in "Fray," the fourth season's second episode, when Ethan was given the job of finding the lake. The show still hasn't explained what the lake does, but a character is now searching for it on screen. The answer is therefore more likely to come through Ethan's search than through anyone's theories.

Ethan is not the only resident tied to the lake. Victor has a drawing of it in his room at the Colony House, according to Primetimer. Ethan dreamed of a place that already meant something to another person in town. How Victor came to know the lake has not been determined. The lake now holds two questions at once: what it is, and what Victor knows about it. In this last stretch of the show, several questions may share a single answer, and the lake and Victor may turn out to be one of them.

The two landmarks do not carry equal weight in the story. The lake has a dream, an errand and a second resident behind it, while the lighthouse is so far mainly the other half of a question about the curse. For an ending built around where the characters arrive, that difference matters. Ethan is already looking for the lake, so the finale would have a hard time leaving it unexplained. The lighthouse may get only as much explanation as the curse requires, which would make it the half of this mystery that fans keep arguing over longest.

Credit: Mjolnir47542 / MGM+ via From Wiki (Fandom)

Every night in Fromville, residents rely on talismans whose origins nobody can explain. The person or force that crafted the charms, and the source of their power, both lack an answer, according to FandomWire. ScreenRant movie and TV news editor Matthew Rudoy counts the makers and the power source as still unknown as well. Residents would have a hard time rebuilding or repairing a protection whose source they can't name. The charms' origin is therefore a stake in the ending and not a piece of trivia.

Part of the talisman's mystery has already been solved on screen. The meaning of the show's recurring symbols remains on the list of open questions, but ScreenRant reports that Tabitha realized in season 4 that the talisman symbol depicts her and Jade. That answer ties the town's protection to two of its current residents. It still doesn't say who put their image on the charms or why.

The question became urgent in the season 4 finale, when the Man in Yellow took the charms away. Disguised as Sophia, he removed talismans and threw them down a Faraway Tree, according to Reel 360 and ComicBook.com. It isn't clear whether she took most of them or all of them, so the final season also has to settle whether any protection is left. An enemy who went to the trouble of removing the charms likely saw them as a real obstacle, and that makes the question of who built them more pressing.

The red lightning looks like the wildest riddle here, but viewers saw when it began. FandomWire lists what sets it off as unanswered, while ScreenRant places its start right after the bottle tree was pulled up. The show hasn't explained how the tree and the storm are connected. The lightning was not the only upheaval, though. Pulling up the tree also turned the daylight to darkness and shook the ground, and Tabitha and Jade ended up stuck underground in the caves. The lightning is probably one part of that wider disruption, and the show may explain it along with the rest. Sophia reacted to the tree's removal with glee, a sign that worse may be coming for the residents.

The ending doesn't need to decode the lightning so much as account for it: who built the town's defenses, and what the red lightning means for the residents now that those defenses have been taken.

Fromville's daily workings remain as unexplained as its deepest secrets. The exits, the spiders, the music box storyline and the source of the town's electricity and supplies all stay murky, according to FandomWire. The explanations for the electricity and fuel that most Reddit $RDDT users accept are fan opinion, and the show has never confirmed them. Questions such as these rarely move the plot forward. Their answers still decide whether the town feels like a built world or an improvised one, and a final season that skips them risks the second.

Residents receive some necessities and not others, and nobody has explained why. Whatever keeps the town supplied has not brought medical care, because there are no doctors, hospitals or surgeons in town, according to TV Insider. That turns a logistics question into a story question. Any account of how the residents get their supplies would also have to explain why medical care is missing. Such an answer would say something about the nature of the place where these people are trapped.

What the town knows about the Faraway Trees begins with one man. Residents use the name Victor Kavanaugh gave the hollow-trunked bottle trees, and Victor is the town's longest-tenured resident, according to TV Insider. No one in town has seen more of the place than he has. The trees' secret is therefore partly a question of how much Victor knows and how much of it he shares with the others.

The trees may also reach back further than Victor. TV Insider reports that no connection between the trees and the town's former residents has been uncovered yet. If that link exists, the trees belong to the town's history as well as its geography, and to the larger story of who came before and what happened to them.

The power supply is the question the finale can most safely leave to fans, because it has no bearing on whether anyone can get out. The trees and the exits are a different matter. They bear on escape itself and on what the town's longest-tenured resident has kept to himself.

The last season needs to explain the trees, and it can leave the electricity a mystery.

Lady Gaga Hits a Yoga Class in Slingback Heels

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

You will rarely catch her in a pair of flats

These days, many seem to be steering away from high heels and challenging the Carrie Bradshaw mindset, opting instead for comfier silhouettes, like ballet flats or opera shoes. Even heels themselves are getting more sensible: think kitten heels rather than teetering stilettos. But Lady Gaga rarely swims with the current.

Since the dawn of her career, the Grammy-winning pop star has worn shoes that seem to defy the laws of physics. She hits the streets in heel-less Nortek Tatehana pointe shoes and sky-high leather Pleasers. Her “Bad Romance” music video helped solidify Alexander McQueen’s scythe-shaped Armadillo shoes as an accessory icon. Sometimes, her heels are even covered in meat. So, it should come as no surprise that, in Gaga’s world, even a workout class calls for a fresh pair of heels.

On Tuesday afternoon, the “Abracadabra” singer was seen heading into a yoga class in Mill Valley, California, while in an easy all-black fit composed of your standard workout fare: a baggy T-shirt and joggers paired with a black baseball cap, plus a scrunchie on her wrist to help pull back her silvery hair. But in true Gaga fashion, she traded workout shoes for a pair of pointy black slingbacks, which gave the casual athleisure look a chic, witchy effect.

This is one of the first times that Gaga has been spotted in public since she and her fiancé, Michael Polansky, welcomed their first child together in June. And while Gaga’s life (and sleep schedule) has surely changed quite dramatically in these past few months, clearly her fun approach to style has not.

ZIM (ZIM) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Premium expedited services and high-value cargo drove a sharp profit jump

By Motley Fool Transcribing – Nov 18, 2026 at 1:11PM EST

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

ZIM (ZIM +1.21%) reported quarterly financial results and raised full-year performance guidance for the second time in 2026. Management reported a strategic focus on profitable trade lanes between Asia and North America, specifically expanding premium expedited services to the U.S. West Coast. The company reported a decline in vessel operating costs driven by fleet modernization and the introduction of larger, more fuel-efficient ships. Management stated that the current network strategy prioritizes high-value cargo categories, including e-commerce, AI data center components, and pharmaceuticals, while capping volumes assigned to standard annual contracts. The company confirmed it is continuing to engage with regulatory authorities regarding its pending merger with Hapag-Lloyd, which is currently targeted for completion in the first quarter of 2027.

Operator: Hello, everyone. Thank you for joining us, and welcome to the ZIM Integrated Shipping Services Third Quarter 2026 Financial Results Conference Call. [Operator Instructions] I will now hand the conference over to Elana Holzman, Head of Investor Relations. Elana, please go ahead.

Elana Holzman: Thank you, operator, and welcome to ZIM's Third Quarter 2026 Financial Results Conference Call. Joining me on the call today are Chen Lichtenstein, ZIM's President and CEO; and Sami Jubran, ZIM's CFO. Before we begin, I would like to remind you that during the course of this call, we will make forward-looking statements regarding expectations, predictions, projections or future events or results. We believe that our expectations and assumptions are reasonable. We wish to caution you that such statements reflect only the company's current expectations and that actual events or results may differ, including materially.

You are kindly referred to consider the risk factors and cautionary language described in the documents the company filed with the Securities and Exchange Commission, including our 2025 annual report on Form 20-F filed with the SEC on March 9, 2026. We undertake no obligation to update these forward-looking statements. We would also like to remind you that during this call, we will not be able to address any questions relating to our pending merger transaction with Hapag-Lloyd. At this time, I would like to turn the call over to ZIM's CEO, Chen Lichtenstein. Chen?

Chen Lichtenstein: Thank you, Elana. Welcome, everyone. Since joining ZIM, my priority has been very clear: to capitalize fully on the current market opportunities by proactive allocation of the company's resources. We optimize every voyage, we maximize profitability, and we give exceptional attention to every detail to maximize also return to our shareholders. Today, we will discuss the third quarter performance, current market conditions, and we will provide an update on the outlook for the remainder of the year. We're really pleased to report industry-leading profitability, both in quarter 3 and the year-to-date.

The results demonstrate the strength of the differentiated business model of ZIM, leading position in key trades, especially the Transpacific, and the ability to respond quickly to changing market conditions. If we move on to Page 3 in the presentation, I'll just touch on a few of the points on this page. So revenues of $2.7 billion, 53% higher than the prior year quarter. Q3 adjusted EBITDA of $1.3 billion with an EBITDA margin of 49% and adjusted EBIT for the quarter of $993 million with a margin of 37%, which is at the top of the companies in this space.

Quarter 3 net income of -- adjusted net income of $717 million and cash flow generated from operations of $1.24 billion. Net cash position has increased to $3.29 billion, and the net leverage ratio is at 0.8x EBITDA. In fact, with the guidance, the updated guidance that we will be providing, one can calculate the net leverage ratio further reduction to a very low number by the end of the year. We're further increasing our guidance, which we'll touch on in a second. We've been capitalizing on our most profitable trades. So we launched or are in the process of launching the ZX3, which is our third premium expedited service from Asia to the West Coast of the United States.

And we've launched the Falcon, which is providing faster transit time from China to Argentina and Southern Brazil. We also continue to secure additional newbuild capacity at attractive costs, and we have today a total pipeline of 60% of our existing fleet capacity. Long-term attractive LNG supply agreement is a key part of our cost trajectory, which is going down. We have today the largest proportion of the fleet, which is LNG-powered, and we're very happy with the supply agreement, long-term supply agreement, which is in place. We're also declaring a dividend for the third quarter of $2.01 per share. If we flick over to the next page, we can see the key market freight rates.

This is market, not us. We can see that the trajectory of 2026, which is clearly different from the trajectory that we've seen in prior years. And it, of course, connects to the congestion in the ports, especially in China, to the situation earlier in the year in the Panama Canal and obviously, the Hormuz and Bab-el-Mandeb congestion. So what we see, if we look into West Coast -- U.S. West Coast -- Asia to U.S. West Coast and Asia to U.S. East Coast trajectories, we see also very high conditions, very strong conditions. And unlike in prior years when there was a reduction at the end of the summer, we don't see that this year.

We do see some softening in recent weeks. We think it's related to people prolonging their orders before Christmas and the end of year, so they don't want to get products into the U.S. during this time. And there is an expectation that the market will pick up again in January before the Chinese New Year, which is a bit earlier this year. Moving on to Page 5. This is the page where we discuss our revised guidance. We provided guidance in August, revised it once before, and now it's a second revision upwards.

So we have a guidance for the end of year 2026 of EBITDA of USD 3.3 billion to USD 3.5 billion for adjusted EBITDA and adjusted EBIT of $2 billion to $2.2 billion. This implies, of course, if you take the adjusted EBITDA or EBIT and deduct what we have already delivered by the end of Q3, it gives a midpoint projection of EBITDA for Q4 of $1.27 billion and midpoint projection for adjusted EBIT in Q4 of $943 million, which is 70x what we delivered in the same period of Q4 last year and 4x the EBITDA that was delivered in Q4 of last year. If we move on to the next page, Page 6.

This is the dividend calculation. So we're declaring $2.01 per share. This is calculated as a total of $243 million, which is 30% of the 9 months 2026 aggregate net income. And this is reported net income, which is somewhat higher than the adjusted net income, so of $808 million. We have the record date, the payable date on this page. This dividend is declared according to our policy, which is consistently applied over the years. If we look into Page 7, a quick update on the pending transaction with Hapag-Lloyd. Hapag provided an update a few days ago when they issued their results for the third quarter. So we basically repeat the same messages here.

Avelo Airlines Launches 16 New Routes In 4 Weeks: See Full List Now

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Newish airlines tend to grow strongly, benefiting from the revenue, unit cost, market share, and competitive advantages of doing so. For example, five-year-old Breeze Airways' passenger traffic grew by 47% in the 12 months to August. In distinct contrast, Avelo Airlines' passenger numbers fell by 15%.

Avelo has been struggling recently as it seeks to find what works. As such, it has cut many routes, including its entire international operation, and removed multiple served airports from its map. However, the airline, whose fleet consists of 17 Boeing 737-700s and 737-800s, will launch 16 new routes in November and December. Still, compared to the same two months in 2025, 30+ routes have been removed.

Analysis of the airline's schedule submission to Cirium Diio has been explored. This compared the budget operator's network between January 2025 and October 2026 with what is set to operate in November 2026. The process found that the following ten routes will launch. This includes a return to the Caribbean. However, as the two routes will be to Aguadilla, Puerto Rico, they'll be domestic. Aguadilla is a brand-new destination for Avelo.

Half of the entries will be from McKinney (DTX), where Avelo's latest base just opened. Located around 34 miles (54 km) north of downtown Dallas, McKinney's passenger terminal opened on November 11. Not surprisingly, that was exactly when the ultra-low-cost carrier Avelo began flying from the airport. In doing so, the carrier now serves three airports in the Lone Star State: Dallas/Fort Worth, McKinney, and Houston Intercontinental.

McKinney is near Dallas, Concord is near Charlotte, and Lakeland is between Orlando and Tampa

Initially, Avelo had one aircraft based at McKinney. However, that'll jump to two this month and, eventually, three. When a trio of aircraft are stationed there, nearly a third of the budget carrier's fleet will be there, which perhaps illustrates the challenges that it has faced.

Simple Flying's Live Flight Tracker shows that the N801XT and N803XT, both of which are 737-800s, will be there this week, although the specific airframes may change in the future. Generally, each aircraft has four legs per day. For example, on November 19, the 20.8-year-old N803XT, which is ex-Turkish Airlines, will fly McKinney-Orlando-McKinney-Las Vegas-McKinney.

As part of the announcement, Allegiant will pull out of Chicago Midway. Find out more now!

They will all be from McKinney and, like the five mentioned earlier, will be brand-new markets. Significantly, it'll be the first time that Avelo has flown to Denver, albeit only with one route and four weekly departures. It'll mark Avelo's return to Colorado after last flying there in September 2024 from Burbank to Colorado Springs.

Previously, the airline also flew from Burbank to Grand Junction and from Burbank and Las Vegas to Fort Collins. The new route contributes to Avelo having a record number of flights to Atlanta, the world's busiest airport for passengers but not for flights

However, context is needed. Only three markets will exist: Lakeland, McKinney, and Tweed New Haven. Previously, Avelo flew there from Wilmington, Delaware. Things are not this simple, as it appears that Lakeland-Atlanta service will end in early January 2027. No flights are scheduled beyond there, but that might change.

US Department of Transportation (DOT) information currently extends to August 2026. In the 12 months to that summer month, Avelo transported nearly 2.3 million round-trip passengers. Compared to the prior 12 months, traffic fell by 15%. While not a like-for-like comparison, Breeze grew by 47%.

Avelo's average load factor is 77.7%. While that is low compared to many budget operators around the world, it is relatively normal for US airlines. As the number of seats for sale fell by 16%, the smaller reduction in traffic (-15%) means that the load factor improved to the highest level since the 12 months to August 2023. What is not known is how fares changed.

Will Wright's next simulation will use AI to mine content from your memories

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

With MyMems, Wright says he wants to “turn the simulation inward.”

The computer gaming world hasn’t heard much from Will Wright (SimCity, The Sims) since the underwhelming release of Spore in 2008 and Wright’s subsequent departure from Maxis owner EA. Now, well over 15 years later, Wright is starting to promote his next digital simulation, which will use generative AI technology to craft a game-like experience from players’ memories.

MyMems, which is slated to release next year, differentiates itself from Wright’s previous projects by “turn[ing] the simulation inward,” as Wright’s Gallium Studios and partner Artificial Agency put it in a press release this morning. The game will mine memories provided by the player to create digital people that can then form teams to solve puzzles in the game world.

“If my Grandmother ended up in a Grand Slam match against Serena Williams, who would win?” Wright says by way of example in the release. “If I had to build a bridge across a short chasm, which three people from my life would I choose to help me?”

In an accompanying announcement video, Wright talks about how the “plausible behavior” in a game like The Sims still didn’t effectively capture the nuances of people he encounters in real life. “It was nowhere near them understanding my claustrophobia or why my sister and I fought so much… We’re actually taking parts of your life, your memories, your experiences, people you know, and turning that into a game that’s very personal.”

Wright said MyMems will be able to extract generalized themes from your memories into self-contained areas that Wright compared to the various parts in a theme park: Sports, vacations, injuries, memories, and deaths could all be the basis for their own zones to play in. Players will also have the option to create worlds based on shared memories with family, friends, and co-workers, for example.

Since this is 2026, it shouldn’t be surprising that MyMems is making heavy use of generative AI (with a “proprietary behavior engine” on top) to transform your typed-up memories into simulated locales and characters. In the announcement video, Wright praises the “entirely open-ended” AI systems Gallium is using “to extract from the stories and whatnot based upon the memories you put into the game.”

But Wright’s focus on mining memories for simulation content predates the current generative AI boom. What has now become MyMems started off as Proxi, a mobile simulation that was first announced in 2018 as “a game of self-discovery, a game where we actually uncover the hidden you.” But after years of pre-production work (as outlined in an excellent Vulture piece earlier this year), Wright’s Gallium Studios finally ran out of funding in late 2024, leading to mass layoffs for the small team and an uncertain future for the project.

Now the idea has been revived thanks in large part to a partnership with AI middleware firm Artificial Agency. Until now, that company has mainly been focused on agentic systems that can create “virtual players” to playtest a game or serve as a partner for human players.

Now, Artificial Agency’s marketing says the company’s AI tool will be used to create “a personal behavior model and a knowledge graph” from players’ memories, drawing some unsettling comparisons to big tech surveillance.

“Meta knows what you click,” the company writes. “Netflix knows what you watch. Nobody knows what you remember. MemDNA learns from your memories locally and only with your permission.”

Wright said the partnership with Artificial Agency will let Gallium benefit from “off-the-shelf” AI solutions without having to “become an AI research company” in its own right. He also said the collaboration will help limit costs by providing a cheaper option than the “really expensive” frontier models that might be overkill for a project like this.

“Right now AI has opened a vast new design area and I’m enjoying, you know, exploring that as a designer,” Wright said.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Is Apple the safe haven investors think it is? Here's a way to trade it with less risk

📅 2026-11-18  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

For months, Apple has been the undisputed haven of the technology sector. As the industry grappled with the brutal reality of Nvidia's post-Huang future, investors blindly rotated into Apple, comforting themselves with the narrative that the Cupertino giant was uniquely immune to speculative overspending on artificial intelligence.

While peers bled capital into server farms and data centers. At almost 37x forward earnings estimates, the stock is now priced for perfection, fully valued at precisely the moment the rest of the market is beginning to see multiples contract aggressively. Investors tend to huddle in the shelter of perceived safe havens, but that perceived safety can crack quickly in an adverse news cycle, as Nvidia shareholders recently learned.

One part of the news cycle finally caught up to Apple.

We've recently witnessed a bizarre parade of catastrophic system failures: the massive Microsoft outage, the FAA grounding flights, and cascading disruptions across major banking networks. Through it all, Apple was immune. Until today, when a widespread outage struck, knocking out iCloud, Apple Pay, and core support systems. The official explanation? Yet another internal update gone wrong.

It's undeniably odd how all these major tech infrastructure failures are being chalked up to routine upgrades, all clustering around the exact same narrow timeframe. Without venturing too far into the conspiratorial, the sheer coincidence of these cascading digital blackouts suggests more systemic fragility than we may have realized. That's one thing at less than 10x forward earnings, as Nvidia is, or even at a market multiple. But Apple is twice as expensive as the S&P.

Technically, Apple is balancing on a knife's edge. Shares have been relentlessly hugging the upper end of several channel-based technical indicators, such as the Bollinger Band. Apple has been priced at a steep premium for being immune to what ails other struggling stocks, but is now demonstrating similar vulnerabilities. Investors should ask themselves: what's the upside? That Apple trades at more than 40x forward earnings? 45x? Even at that rich multiple, that would represent only 18-19% upside from here. If the market suddenly decided that Apple's premium to the market would be just 50%, instead of 100%, that would be less than 28x forward earnings, a nearly 27% haircut to the current share price.

Despite the high multiple, options prices are low versus the market. Market implied volatility is well above average (around the 93rd percentile). Apple implied volatility is actually just below the 1-year average. The price of index options is in the 98th percentile relative to Apple options.

Because the stock is nominally expensive (translation? The share price is high in dollar terms), buying outright naked puts is highly capital-intensive. This is exactly where a put spread makes sense. By buying an at-the-money or slightly out-of-the-money put and simultaneously selling a lower-strike put, traders can dramatically reduce the upfront capital cost. More importantly, this structure blunts the negative impact of theta, or time decay, ensuring you don't bleed premium while waiting for the technical breakdown to materialize. Furthermore, taking advantage of the natural put skew—where further out-of-the-money puts trade at relatively higher implied volatility—lets you finance your bearish bet efficiently by selling that inflated premium.

Apple's historic premium is built on a foundation of immunity to the chaos infecting the rest of the market. Today's outage proved the company may be more vulnerable than investors appreciate.

Celtic legend tells protesters there is ‘better way forward’ as he reveals Michael Nicholson chat

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Paul McStay believes major shareholder Dermot Desmond is not planning to walk away “anytime soon” from Celtic as he underlined the importance of fans having a say in the running of the club.

The Celtic legend is a Celtic Season Ticket Alliance Trust ambassador and is back in Scotland from his base in Australia to launch his autobiography and raise awareness of Willie Haughey’s independent supporter initiative.

Did you know with a Digital Subscription to The Scotsman, you can get unlimited access to the website including our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

Haughey has pledged to buy £2 million worth of shares in the first instance if 10,000 supporters sign up. The principal ambition is to install a fans’ representative on the club’s board.

According to McStay, almost 5,000 of Celtic’s 54,000 season ticket holders have joined to date amid continued civil unrest at the club.

McStay, who plans to attend Saturday’s home game against St Mirren, is a link between the board and fans and witnessed the discontent first hand when he was invited to Parkhead last season to unfurl the title flag.

He cites the spirit of the team that overcame Hearts on the final day of the league season in 1986, when he was in his pomp in midfield, as the inspiration behind the Alliance’s efforts to reach a long-term goal.

“I go back to the ‘86 year, when we won the league,” he said. “Hearts were there dominant in pole position. If we wanted to have success and win that league as players and manager Davie Hay, we have to stay focused on what our objective was. It was win each game at a time. With the Alliance, our objective is 10,000 season ticket holders signed up and Willie Haughey says he will put money in and buy shares.

“I understand the frustrations last year, because of the European situation and Kairat,” he added. “The boycotts, the protests. I understand that. But I think there is a better way to move forward, a more professional, business-like way and try and get someone on the board to talk and communicate and provide sports opinion.”

He revealed he has discussed these aims with under-fire chief executive Michael Nicholson at a recent match.

“I go to games,” he said. “Last year I was back and invited as a guest to raise the flag so I was here when all the protests and boycotts were happening. The Celtic supporters want the best for their club but it is slightly different to what I think should be done. Again, that is about opinions. Even a couple of weeks ago I was at a game and I bumped into Michael – I am quite happy to give my opinions, whether that is in the dressing room with fellow players at the time or as captain talking to Fergus (McCann) or Billy McNeill or Liam Brady.”

An agm is scheduled for the start of next month. Last year’s meeting had to be cut short after Ross Desmond, Dermot’s son, was harangued while reading a pre-prepared statement from his father, who is not expected to attend this year's meeting either.

“You ask about Dermot and his position there,” said McStay. “He has put shares in the club. But to me I don’t think he is going to go anywhere soon. To me, this is a long-term objective. It might take time but we have to focus on what we are doing and build up support within our organisation to build up equity to give Celtic supporters a voice on the board. I think that will help things.”

Mary Miss’s Long Fight to Keep Public Art Alive

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

In the early decades of postwar America, public art tended to be seen as the pallid stepchild of architecture and design. Steel, bronze, or stone abstractions sited in front of modernist buildings were denigrated as “plop art,” or even more damningly as “the turd in the plaza.” And, accordingly, public artists were relegated to the province of decoration or historical commemoration while public responses to their art ranged from indifference to outrage over the expenditure of public money.

Then, in the 1980s, public art was jolted by a revolution. Rebelling against the tradition of the static monument, a group of young artists aspired instead to explore how art in public spaces could encourage connection, reflection, and interaction among viewers who didn’t necessarily have an art education. They created mazes, placed mirrors in the landscape, and drew lines in the snow. An extension of the boundary-breaking experiments of avant-garde artists of the 1970s, this new public art slowly began to gain acceptance from funders, developers, government officials, and general audiences. Related Articles Walid Raad's Way of Working Is Inspired by the Mirrors in Vampire Movies Art in America Celebrates Fall "Icons" Issue with Talk Featuring William T. Williams and Nari Ward

Among the most adventurous of these pioneers was Mary Miss. Raised in the American West, she was interested in the tensions between rural and urban landscapes, the built and unbuilt environment, and the ways in which people navigate their path through the spaces of the modern world. By 1988, she had created an impressive roster of temporary and permanent projects that challenged the conventional definitions of public art. That year saw the creation of one of her most celebrated works, a waterside park titled South Cove (1984–87) in the newly created Battery Park City development at the bottommost tip of Manhattan. Created on a former landfill site, this work incorporates natural plantings, rocks, walkways, trellises, wooden pilings, a circular jetty, and an overlook that cantilevers over the edge of the Hudson River. Brilliantly weaving together architecture, landscape, and references to Manhattan’s maritime history, it remains a much-loved intervention in the urban landscape.

The year she made South Cove, Miss sat down with curator Patterson Sims at the 92nd Street Y for a wide-ranging discussion of her work and her views on public art. When he prodded her to speculate on the future of this new way of creating art for the public, Miss replied, “I think we’re just beginning to see what the possibilities are.” Then, reflecting on the support provided for such projects by the National Endowment for the Arts, she added, “I think they’ve started something that can’t stop.”

The irony of this faith in the NEA is not lost on Miss today. This spring, sitting in the Tribeca studio she has occupied since 1970, she recalled the enthusiasm of her younger self with what seemed like envy. “I was still feeling so optimistic,” she says. “I hadn’t been beaten down yet.”

Now a seasoned practitioner, her dedication to public art and dialogue remains intact, but has been tempered by the realities of politics, economics, and societal change. She has endured bureaucratic meddling; watched lofty rhetoric butt up against budgets, architects, and physical and technical limitations; experienced the shrinking and politicization of public money; and seen one of her signature pieces dismantled. In the course of her long career, she’s worked on numerous proposals that haven’t come to fruition, and at times put up with nonpayment and the theft of her ideas.

Yet, while these conditions might have deterred a less determined artist, Miss has responded by adapting: She shifts gears, rethinks her basic premises, and finds a new way. Her current project, “City as Living Laboratory” (CaLL), takes the idea of public art away from its dependence on gatekeepers and experts and returns it to the communities in which it’s created—a testament to her resilience and her remarkable capacity for reinvention.

IT TAKES A SPECIAL kind of person to operate successfully—and continually—as a public artist. Miss was born in New York City in 1944, but spent a peripatetic childhood moving around the country, with extended stays in Colorado and Florida as the family followed her father, who was in the military. She recalls a formative childhood memory of sitting in the back of an old Chevy and watching the scenery spin by. Fences laid across the fields whizzed by in a fine filigree and then gradually disappeared as the urban landscape took over. Such experiences sparked an enduring interest in vernacular forms and quotidian materials. They also provided a metaphor she has frequently invoked of public art as a wide net that stretches across barriers and connects people.

After getting a BA from the University of California, Santa Barbara, Miss came East and enrolled in graduate school at the Maryland Institute College of Art in Baltimore. However, she did not find the faculty very receptive to her interests, so she spent a year out of residence in Colorado. There, she did outdoor projects that contained the germ of her subsequent public work before returning East to New York City in 1968. It was an exciting time to be an artist: While the city itself was struggling economically, cheap rents made it a paradise for the era’s avant-garde. Miss fell in with a group of young women, among them Hannah Wilke, Elizabeth Murray, Nancy Holt, and Alice Aycock, who were challenging the orthodoxies of a male-dominated art world that was still fanning the flames of formalism. Miss recalls, “We figured, OK, you guys aren’t going to play with us. We’ll figure out another way.”

Miss’s early works, extending ideas of the ordinary yet defamiliarized spaces she had begun to explore in Colorado, were constructions using common materials that challenged viewers’ perceptions and sense of space. Some were indoor sculptures comprising bits of rope, ladders, stairs, or barbed wire. Others, using similar materials, were set outside in open fields and on hillsides. She generally eschewed urban contexts, and her modest approach was a marked contrast to the massive works of earth-movers like Michael Heizer and Robert Smithson, for whom an unpeopled landscape provided an empty canvas to be reshaped into a giant sculpture. Instead, Miss’s works were designed to alter viewers’ perspectives as they moved through and around the site, discovering unexpected vistas and pathways.

One work from this period, Perimeters/Pavilions/Decoys (1977–78), temporarily installed across four acres at the Nassau County Museum in Roslyn, New York, embodied the kind of paradoxes Miss was pursuing. Three towers and two semicircular mounds drew viewers forward, but the bulk of the work was not visible on the surface of the land. Instead, a ladder between the mounds invited viewers underground, where they encountered a series of corridors, doors, and slotlike windows.

The work caught the eye of the formidable critic Rosalind Krauss, whose now-classic 1979 essay “Sculpture in the Expanded Field” helped launch Miss into the limelight. In it, Krauss laid out a framework for understanding the movement of sculpture beyond the realm of the object and into an entanglement with architecture and landscape. She opened the essay with a description of Perimeters/Pavilions/Decoys, using it to illustrate the deconstruction of the idea of sculpture and its replacement, the idea of the field. In her typically abstruse fashion, Krauss noted, “Thus the field provides both for an expanded but finite set of related positions for a given artist to occupy and explore, and for an organization of work that is not dictated by the conditions of a particular medium.”

Miss was grateful for the attention, but remarks wryly, “I don’t know what she was interested in, and I didn’t know then what she was talking about. And she obviously didn’t really mean the ‘expanded’ field, because as soon as I started doing that, I went off her radar.” In fact, while Krauss’s essay mapped a set of formal relations, Miss was expanding the field of art to address human concerns.

The artist’s attention shifted from rural to urban settings, particularly to New York City, where she lived and worked. Her projects involved deep research into a site’s history, context, and present use, and these issues helped shape her concepts. For instance, when she was invited to work with the architects who were renovating the Union Square subway station in 1992, she delved into the archeology of the station and discovered how greatly original features like stairs, lighting fixtures, mosaic tiles, and turnstiles had fallen into disrepair or been altered or replaced over the years. She created 125 red framing elements that marked these changes, highlighting historical details that passersby might otherwise miss. At Battery Park City’s South Cove, pilings, blue ships’ lanterns hung on wooden lampposts, and native rocks and plants bring New York City’s residents back in touch with the area’s previous life as an important port. In a playful touch, the shape of the overlook platform deliberately mimics the crown of the Statue of Liberty, which is visible across the water.

In 1989, Miss embarked on a pivotal project—one that recently made headlines. She was invited by the Des Moines Art Center to create something in the nearby Greenwood Pond. Once an important community gathering place, the pond had deteriorated and become overrun with green algae. She started by researching the site, speaking with local ecologists, historians, and Meskwaki Nation elders. Miss discovered that the site had once contained a wetland, and that it, like most of Iowa’s wetlands, had been drained and tilled for agriculture. She also investigated the history and lives of the Meskwaki tribe, which had once occupied this land.

From that research, Miss decided to restore the pond while transforming it into a kind of demonstration wetland, using forms that echoed Native American lodges and earthen mounds. This work, titled Greenwood Pond: Double Site (1989–96), comprised a network of paths, canopies, and mounds, including a wooden pavilion following the curve of the shore as well as an elevated lookout and a walkway that plunged below the surface, allowing visitors to sit beneath the water level. As a newly restored wetland, it welcomed birds, insects, wildlife, and native plants in an educational display of biodiversity.

For this project, Miss collaborated with a wide range of community members and groups. She reflects that Greenwood Pond: Double Site “was kind of a fulcrum for me, because I was able to have a scientist, the Science Center, the Founders Garden Club, the Parks Department, the museum, all in conversation. It was a different way of working.”

For Jenny Dixon, director emerita of the Noguchi Museum and the inaugural director of the Public Art Fund, this “different way of working” is what makes Miss “a major figure in this field.” Dixon, who has known Miss since the 1970s, adds that “you just can’t make a piece of public art in your studio. There has to be another piece to it, whether it’s community, architecture, the space, the site, the environment. Mary’s not one-dimensional.”

With Greenwood Pond: Double Site, Miss began to shift from her earlier focus on land and history to the kinds of ecological issues that subsequently have become her primary focus. “In the wake of climate change,” she remarks, “artists need to help redefine nature. I’ve long claimed that the city is nature and we are living in it.”

But the project was also pivotal for a less happy reason: While the work was enjoyed by the public and maintained by the Art Center through various administrations, by 2024 it had fallen into disrepair. Kelly Baum, the Art Center’s director, informed Miss that the work would need to be dismantled because the Art Center did not have the $8 million necessary for restoration and future maintenance. A public uproar and lawsuit ensued as Miss attempted to force the museum to raise the funds to save the work. In the end, a settlement was reached: The work was taken down, and Miss was awarded $900,000, a portion of which she earmarked for a newly established fund to support threatened site-specific art works.

AT LEAST GREENWOODPOND: DOUBLE SITE was realized and lasted for 29 years. For Miss, the 1990s were marked by increasing frustration as many projects that had consumed considerable time and effort never came to fruition. These included projects for New York City; Los Angeles; St. Paul; St. Louis; and Long Beach, California. Many other projects that did see the light of day were then altered or diminished by the architects and planners with whom she was working.

Then came September 11, 2001. Miss’s studio was in the shadow of the Twin Towers. She recalls that her place “was covered with dust because the windows had been open. We had to come in and clean everything.” Deeply aware of the need for collective mourning, she soon came up with the idea of creating a temporary project to establish places where people could gather and lay flowers. Working with architects Elliott Maltby and Victoria Marshall, Miss named the work Moving Perimeter: A Wreath for Ground Zero (2002). She was struck by the motley nature of the temporary barriers and fences—composed of wood, chain link, concrete, and pipe—that kept out people making a pilgrimage to the site. Her idea was to paint some of these blue and replace others with new sections of fencing, seating, planting, and blue lights in order to create a movable perimeter that would grow denser and smaller as the exclusion zone decreased. It would operate as a place for people to gather and connect and share the profound sense of loss that was enveloping the city.

After years of struggling with bureaucracies, distant deadlines, and ultimately rejections, this project was an eye-opener for Miss. Although it was never realized, it still allowed her to rethink what public art could be: If it was temporary, it could be executed quickly and flexibly. And it would help if it relied on willing participants rather than reluctant bureaucrats to give the work meaning. “Wait a second,” Miss says she realized at one point. “There’s a way I can function instead of just waiting to get something built. You just need to take charge.”

While she was still working on some large-scale permanent projects during this period, she began to think in terms of interactive, collaborative, open-ended works that left a light footprint. This culminated in 2010 with the inauguration of CaLL, based on a concept that went all the way back to her idea of art as a net stretched across communities or even whole cities. She describes CaLL as “the idea that artists, working in collaboration with scientists, engineers, planners, and communities, can have an essential role to play in addressing the pressing environmental issues of our times.”

Over the past 15 years, CaLL has evolved into a series of different practices, including workshops, urban walks, programs, and community-initiated projects. The most ambitious of these is an initiative with the city of Milwaukee called WaterMarks (2015–). Working with ecologist Eric Sanderson, she has focused on the city’s complex relationship with water. A source of life, water also poses dangers from flooding, contamination, and aging infrastructures. WaterMarks involves going into neighborhoods to explore their specific relationship to water, engaging local residents, artists, and public and private institutions along the way. The project coalesces around a set of markers, 25-foot poles wrapped with solar panels and topped by illuminated blue letters chosen by the community.

These poles map out the nexus for educational and artistic projects that center on local stewardship of water. For instance, at Milwaukee’s Acosta Middle School, students chose the letter A for their marker, standing both for Acosta and, in this Latino area, agua, or water. In a series of walks with experts through the neighborhood, students learned about the area’s water history, its green infrastructure, and the need for storm water management in an urban environment. A green space created on a remediated slice of abandoned industrial wasteland became the Green Tech Station, designated with the letter G. This is a site for development, demonstration, and education about green infrastructure projects relating to the management of invasive species, pollutants, and flooding. The letter H designates the Harbor View neighborhood, where enjoyment of water includes activities like swimming, fishing, and kayaking.

So far, WaterMarks has 14 markers in place. Together they form what Miss describes as an atlas of water for the city. Rising above the network of markers is a 350-foot stack in the center of the city’s water treatment plant that has been repurposed as a beacon, with changing colors that warn residents when they need to restrict their water usage to avoid possible sewage overflows into Lake Michigan. “One really wonderful thing that’s come out of this 10-year project,” Miss says, “is that there’s a collective of people who are going to take it forward.”

WaterMarks is the most fully realized of CaLL’s undertakings, but the ideas it exemplifies appear in projects in other cities as well. In Boulder, Colorado, in 2007, Miss worked with climate scientists to map the potential disaster of the long-anticipated “500-year flood” with metal discs on trees, fences, and buildings that charted predicted water levels. In Indianapolis in 2014, Miss worked with local organizations to install along the White River more than 100 markers and mirrors that employed technologies like apps, webcam projections, and websites to provide information on the river’s history and ecology.

Building on Miss’s earlier projects in Manhattan, CaLL has also undertaken several initiatives in New York City. Broadway: 1000 Steps, initiated in 2009, is an ongoing reenvisioning of the city’s longest street as a “green corridor” equipped with hubs that provide text, images, and technology designed to engage the public in dialogues about environmental issues. In 2016, CaLL started Rescuing Tibbetts Brook in the Bronx. This project focuses on a plan to unearth a long-buried stream that now feeds into the sewer system and diverts it to a corridor adjacent to the Major Deegan Expressway, where it can become a new linear park.

Miss is currently working in Corona, Queens, on Cloudburst. This project charts the path taken by water during recent devastating flooding events in this low-lying immigrant neighborhood and helps residents understand the dangers as well as the mitigation efforts being undertaken by the city’s Department of Environmental Protection (DEP). CaLL has organized an advisory committee comprised of local residents and institutions, which will work with the DEP to pinpoint problems and needs. Central to Cloudburst are walks, either led by experts or self-guided, that explore the area’s ecology, specifically its water problems and possible solutions. In a move reminiscent of the markers set up in Milwaukee, beneath trees along the path, CaLL has placed benches with signs that invite passersby to connect to informative recordings via the internet.

But it’s the templates these projects create that are the most meaningful—at least for Olivia Georgia, who has been executive director of CaLL since early in its inception, in 2010. “It’s a model that can be refreshed, enhanced, and amplified by other artists,” she says. “We can’t imagine what the other artists’ projects will be. We set the table for them, but they have to figure out how to use the instruments in the tool kit we have assembled.”

Miss came up as part of a generation benefiting from federal support for the arts; but since that time, the tides have very much turned. She recently had grants canceled by both the National Science Foundation and the National Endowment for the Arts. “They’re saying, ‘No science. No climate change,’” she explains, adding that this backlash makes it all the more important to find self-sustaining ways to help people connect to environmental issues. “I’ve had to really change my practice. I loved doing the Des Moines project. I love doing these projects that cost a lot of money. But now I’m trying to find a different way…. I’ve gone from this young woman who wanted to be acknowledged as an artist and who wanted to be able to make a mark with the work that I did, and I’ve ended up wanting this CaLL work to continue. That’s what I would really love to be able to leave behind.

“I really got beaten down in the ’90s,” she reflects, “and yet I ended up in this place. I did come out from under it. I’m glad I reached my optimism again.”

Ares Capital Turned $10,000 Into Nearly $31,000 Over 10 Years. Can Its Dividend Do It Again?

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The leading BDC is still a reliable income investment for compounding total returns.

10 stocks we like better than Ares Capital ›

Ares Capital (ARCC -0.13%), the largest publicly traded business development company (BDC), pays a massive forward dividend yield of 10.2%. That high yield might seem like a red flag, but it's actually maintained stable or increasing dividends for the past 17 years.

Over the past 10 years, Ares' stock price only rose about 20%. But with reinvested dividends, it delivered a total return of 209% -- which would have turned a $10,000 investment into about $30,900. Should investors count on Ares to replicate those gains over the next ten years?

BDCs provide debt and equity financing to smaller "middle market" businesses that struggle to secure loans from conventional banks because they're considered riskier borrowers. In exchange for taking on more risk, BDCs charge higher interest rates than banks.

Ares has invested in 636 companies across its $29.2 billion portfolio. To reduce its overall risk, it allocates 58% of its portfolio to first-lien secured loans and 4% to second-lien secured loans.

To grow at a steady rate, Ares needs the Fed to keep interest rates in a "Goldilocks" zone between 3% and 4%. Lower rates will help its portfolio companies but reduce its net interest income, while higher rates will hurt its portfolio companies but boost its net interest income.

The Fed funds rate, at 3.75%-4.00%, is currently in that ideal range. But if inflation stays hot, another rate hike could squeeze its portfolio companies.

As a BDC, Ares continually issues new shares to finance its loans and investments. However, it also needs to pay at least 90% of its taxable income as dividends to maintain a lower tax rate. Therefore, most of the profits from Ares' investments flow directly into its massive dividends.

BDCs usually fund their dividends with their net interest income (NII) per share rather than their earnings per share (EPS). Its net asset value (NAV) per share also gives us a clearer view of how its investments are faring, and whether its stock is overvalued relative to those assets.

From 2015 to 2025, Ares' NII per share grew from $1.62 to $2.02, its annual dividends rose from $1.57 to $1.92, and its NAV per share increased from $16.46 to $19.94. At $19, it trades at a discount to its most recent NAV per share of $19.31 in the third quarter of 2026.

Ares' core growth rates seem modest, but they're enough to fund its massive dividends. As long as its NII per share covers its dividends and its stock doesn't become overvalued, it could replicate its impressive gains from the past decade through reinvested, compounded dividends.
