Pellegatti: Calvelli, Amorim, Krösche – why it may have been ‘better to lose against Cagliari’

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Carlo Pellegatti has painted a positive picture regarding AC Milan’s developments, one that puts last season’s collapse into perspective.

Rewinding to the end of May, it felt like Milan’s entire world have caved in. A 2-1 defeat at home to Cagliari – a game that they led in early on – in the final round of the season condemned the Rossoneri to a fifth-placed finish, meaning they missed out on Champions League football again.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

This is after Massimiliano Allegri had the team in a title fight going into the Spring, but ultimately the decline was near-vertical towards the end of the campaign. He paid the price for this, as did the CEO Giorgio Furlani, sporting director Igli Tare and chief scout Geoffrey Moncada.

In his latest column for MilanNews, Carlo Pellegatti has put forward the notion that the defeat to the Sardinians might turn out to be a blessing. Why? Because the reset that followed on a sporting and managerial level seems to already be taking a positive shape.

“Krosche and delight! It’s a somewhat obvious joke, but it captures the expectations of AC Milan fans. When will he arrive? November 27th, as I’ve been told, or in the first ten days of December, or even – I really hope not – January 1st?” he began.

“The difference in dates for the announcement of his new role is by no means insignificant, because the January transfer window becomes a crucial turning point for Milan’s fortunes, both today and in the future.

“The sooner the Head of Football sits down at Casa Milan, the better he can weave his web to strengthen the team in the winter transfer window. This should have been the job of a year ago, but was not accomplished by a shortsighted management team that never understood the difference between costs and investments.

“It’s also a shame that Igli Tare is a person who respects roles and is attentive to internal balances. He had to, and this is his great regret, bypass those who managed Milan, to speak with Gerry Cardinale and explain the importance of the arrival of a striker and a defender, who would have allowed them to be in the Champions League today.

“I’m using a very bold paradox, one I’ve never admitted. If failing to qualify for the Champions League was the reason that pushed Gerry Cardinale to fire the key figures of the three worst and most disastrous years in Milan’s history, then, given the exciting future prospects, it would have been better to lose against Cagliari, which avoided prolonging the agony of our dear old AC Milan. A painful sacrifice that, I hope, will be repaid with success in the years to come.

“With a Cardinale who was too absent and distant, guilty, by his own admission, of having delegated the management of his club to unsuitable people, but who now becomes the protagonist of this new way of operating, reminiscent of the good old days.

“I obviously feel sorry for Tare and Allegri, who, despite having their responsibilities, were victims of a confusing, chaotic, and divisive situation, and through no fault of their own.

“Returning to Krosche, I hope he quickly finds his feet in this Milan adventure that awaits him. I continue to believe that a highly experienced and highly skilled Italian operative, to work alongside the German director, could and can be of help to him and Milan, given the obvious difficulties of integrating into a world that is completely different from the German one.

“Gerry Cardinale and his chief assistant, Massimo Calvelli, are convinced of the wisdom of their choice, after meeting several times with Markus Krosche. I think it’s crucial that he’s not only a skilled market operator, but also a valid on-field contact for Ruben Amorim.

“The Portuguese will then be able to discuss potential choices and decisions with his Head of Football. In short, it was a position in the organisational chart that was culpably neglected, with the results that were seen, until Gerry Cardinale himself imposed Igli Tare, who, in any case, did not have an easy job.

“I mentioned Massimo Calvelli, who, in the media, always appears to be somewhat out of the spotlight. Certainly not in operational terms, because, from what I know, he was the absolute protagonist and architect of transactions that brought approximately €825m into Milan’s coffers.

“He was certainly a skilled architect of the return of Adidas as a shirt sponsor, but also a key figure in the acquisition of Generali, which will give its name to the new stadium, this time with the collaboration, it is said, of Paolo Scaroni.

“Therefore, he is a valuable executive who works behind the scenes, but with excellent results in terms of economic and financial aspects and also for the club’s image.”

Brighton midfielder Yasin Ayari has hinted he could leave the club next summer, with Chelsea and Manchester United monitoring his situation ahead of a potential transfer.

The Sweden international has enjoyed an impressive campaign under Fabian Hurzeler, attracting attention from several European clubs as uncertainty continues to surround his long-term future at the Amex Stadium.

According to a TEAMtalk report, Ayari has refused to commit to anything beyond the current season, and his contract situation is becoming increasingly complicated.

The 23-year-old has scored three goals in nine Premier League appearances this season, including the winning goal against Manchester City. His performances have helped Brighton establish themselves among the division’s leading sides.

The midfielder’s uncertain contractual situation could present an attractive opportunity for interested clubs, with Chelsea and Manchester United among the sides linked with his signature.

AC Milan, AS Roma, Galatasaray and Fenerbahce are also understood to be monitoring developments, potentially creating significant competition for his services.

Brighton are keen to retain an important member of Hurzeler’s squad, but negotiations over new terms have hit snags. The Seagulls also possess an option to extend his existing agreement until 2028.

However, the growing interest in his services could complicate their efforts to secure his future.

Ayari’s technical ability, energy and versatility make him an attractive prospect for Chelsea and United, both of whom could benefit from additional quality in midfield.

With his contract situation approaching a crucial stage, Brighton will be desperate to avoid losing one of their most promising performers without securing an appropriate financial return.

A U.S. Film Incentive Would Help These Three States Most of All

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

New York, New Jersey and Georgia are poised to reap the greatest benefit thanks to unlimited state tax credits.

Hollywood studios are not banking on a U.S. tax incentive just yet. Though President Trump reaffirmed his support for the measure in a Truth Social post on Nov. 13, insiders say there is only a “narrow path” to approving the bill by the end of the year.

Still, supporters are allowing themselves to dream about the potential effect if it does pass, using words like “massive” and “game changer.” Related Stories Alycia Baumgardner vs. Elif Nur Turhan on ESPN Unlimited: Here’s Where to Watch the Title Bout Live Online Nike Drops New Air Jordan 11 Retro 'Green Screen' Sneakers: Here’s Where To Buy a Pair Online

“It’s gonna be a golden age of productions,” says Brian Papworth, executive director of the Coalition for American Production, who has been lobbying for the measure at the Capitol.

Three states — New York, New Jersey and Georgia — offer generous film credits and stand to gain the most, according to several production executives who spoke to Variety. Producers love shooting in those states not because of the credit amount — but because credits are automatic.

California has more production capacity — i.e., crews and stages — than anywhere else in the world and will benefit too, especially as work spills over from an overbooked New York. But it is not producers’ first choice, because it sticks to its $750 million incentive cap.

Georgia has no limit on film rebates, and in New Jersey and New York, the caps are not enforced. New York’s program is budgeted at $800 million a year. But since making the formula more generous last year, the state has blown through that limit — handing out $1.7 billion in credits in the 12 months ending July 1.

New Jersey’s program is also growing fast, and has $3 billion in reserves once the state completes deals with studio partners. Both states pull from future-year allocations when demand exceeds the cap, so producers never have to worry about being turned away. All three states also allow producers to claim “above the line” expenses, such as big-ticket salaries for directors, producers and actors. For the studios, that puts those states in a class by themselves.

Adding a 20% to 30% federal incentive would create an effective subsidy of 50% or more (in New York, more like 60%). That far exceeds the incentives offered in the U.K., Canada and Australia. If the U.S. incentive were to take effect on Jan. 1, 2027, studios would likely start bringing feature films and other productions back by the end of the year.

Filming in New York and New Jersey is already hot, and it’s starting to heat up in Georgia after a few down years. Adding a federal incentive would pour nitroglycerine on the fire — at least in those states. California, meanwhile, belongs to a second tier of states — also including Pennsylvania, Massachusetts, New Mexico and Kentucky — that would not gain as much because their programs are capped.

“A federal credit lifts all boats,” says Ed Lammi, a retired production exec who worked at Sony Pictures TV. “It would lift our boat too. I still think we need to basically eliminate the cap in California.”

California’s program has three major problems in the eyes of studios and producers. First, the state does not borrow from future-year allocations. Second, because of that, productions must compete for a finite amount of credits, which requires a cumbersome application process. And third, the state does not include above-the-line expenses.

Michael Kagan, co-head of unscripted at Range Media Partners, has grown increasingly frustrated as his reality competition show clients have been forced to leave California to work.

“The state hasn’t done a good job of helping preserve these projects,” he says, noting that “American Idol” left for Atlanta and “America’s Got Talent” and “The Voice” are also eyeing a move. “Now there’s three large-scale network competition shows in L.A. There used to be 15 or 20.”

Those shows became eligible for California tax credits last year, but so far only one — Mark Rober’s “Schooled!” — has received them.

Competition shows used to shoot in L.A. by default, Kagan says. “Now when they’re starting, they look at the U.K., Canada, Ireland and Timbuktu.”

A federal incentive would help bring those projects back from overseas — but maybe not to L.A.

“I think it’ll be great in general,” Kagan says. “But I don’t think it’ll be great for the state of California.”

Google is forcing Android app developers to properly support foldables

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Now that the iPhone Duo is foldable is here, Google wants to make sure apps on Android foldables keep up.

Now that the iPhone Duo is foldable is here, Google wants to make sure apps on Android foldables keep up.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Now that Apple’s foldable is here, Google wants to make sure apps on Android foldables are a good experience. Starting in April 2027, Google is going to require Android developers to make sure their apps have a baseline level of support for foldables. If they don’t, developers risk not being able to publish or update their apps or have reduced visibility for their apps on Google Play.

Google says Android apps must meet its “Tier 3 — Adaptive ready” standard. “Apps must fill the available display area without letterboxing or compatibility modes,” Google says. “Apps must preserve state (scroll position, text input, media playback) during rotation, folding/unfolding, and resizing, while supporting multi-window environments.” (The other two tiers are “Adaptive optimized” and “Adaptive differentiated” and take further advantage of the possibilities of foldables.) As of the April 2027 deadline, developers also will have to include three foldable screenshots for their app listings. Developers can start publishing their foldable screenshots today.

Similar requirements will apply to games, though developers have until August 2027 to get their titles ready for Android foldables, Google says. (Google “plans to extend” its requirements to other large-screen devices like tablets and Googlebook laptops in “future phases.”) However, game developers will still need to share three foldable screenshots for app listings starting in April.

The changes are being announced just weeks after Apple launched the iPhone Duo, which already has more than 200,000 optimized apps, though some developers are still figuring out Apple’s foldable form factor.

Marc Andreessen has a surprisingly mundane prediction about AI’s impact

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

When it comes to AI’s potential impact on the economy, Marc Andreessen thinks extreme optimists and extreme pessimists have one thing in common: they are both wrong.  

“Both the utopians and the doomers are going to be disappointed [by] the scope and magnitude of the real-world impact [of AI], and specifically the economic impact,” the prominent venture capitalist said Monday at the Federal Reserve’s AI conference in San Francisco.

Andreessen thinks both camps are overlooking that vast economic sectors—including health care, education, housing, law, and government—are blocked from rapid transformation by heavy regulation, limited competition, and other factors.

While this could limit AI-driven job losses in those sectors, it could also make their services increasingly expensive relative to other goods and services.

“[Health care] is already a fifth of U.S. GDP, and I don’t see why it doesn’t go to a fourth, a third and then half and then ultimately 80%,” Andreessen said.

The implications of this view depend on your values, he added.  

“You might interpret this as good news, because it means the AI revolution is not going to, you know, destroy all the jobs or kill everybody,” he said. “You could also view this as very negative, which is [that] productivity … simply may not grow very much.”

Andreessen is the co-founder and general partner of Silicon Valley venture capital firm Andreessen Horowitz and co-leader of Fed chairman Kevin Warsh’s Productivity and Jobs Task Force.

Andreessen’s comments on Monday marked a change in tone, if not substance, from many of his previous remarks on AI. 

In 2023, he famously argued that “AI will save the world” and that “productivity growth throughout the economy will accelerate dramatically”—leading to higher wages, lower prices and more jobs.

Andreessen has long conditioned his more bullish predictions about AI on the technology being allowed to proliferate freely throughout the economy. His comments on Monday suggest he is less confident about the prospects of that happening.

His argument is rooted in an economic phenomenon known as “Baumol’s cost disease,” which states that as technological advances make some goods and services cheaper to produce, goods and services that are less susceptible to productivity gains become relatively more expensive. This means that rapid technological progress in certain industries could leave Americans devoting an ever-larger share of their incomes to industries that have barely changed.

Fed chairman Warsh reportedly did not respond directly to the comments on Monday, describing AI as a “hinge point for the U.S. economy” and concluding that he is “cautiously optimistic.”

Before taking office, Warsh argued that AI could be a disinflationary force that increases productivity and justifies lower interest rates.
